THESE APPEALS HAVING BEEN HEARD AND RESERVED
FOR JUDGMENT ON 24.03.2026, COMING ON FOR
PRONOUNCEMENT THIS DAY, HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE
D.K. SINGH PRONOUNCED THE FOLLOWING:

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
229
CORAM: HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE D K SINGH
 and
 HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE T.M.NADAF
CAV JUDGMENT
(PER: HON'BLE MR. JUSTICE D K SINGH)
I. PREFACE:
1. The present batch of writ appeals arise out of a common
judgment and order dated 04.07.2025 passed by the learned
Single Judge in W.P.No.50634/2014 and connected matters
concerning the lands acquired under the provisions of the
Karnataka Industrial Areas Development Act, 1966 (for short
‘the KIAD Act’). The land acquisitions were intiated pursuant to
the Framework Agreement (FWA) dated 03.04.1997 executed
between the Government of Karnataka and the appellant-Nandi
Infrastructure Corridor Enterprises Limited (NICE) for
implementation of Bengaluru-Mysuru Infrastructure Corridor
Project (BMICP). Some of the writ appeals are filed by the NICE
and the others are filed by the Karnataka Industrial Areas
Development Board (KIADB) challenging the aforesaid
judgment and order passed by the learned Single Judge.

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
230
II FACTS OF THE CASE:
2. The BMICP was conceived as a major infrastructure
project intended to provide connectivity between Bengaluru and
Mysuru through construction of:
• Five townships;
• An Expressway of approximately 111 kilometres together
with interchanges and toll plazas;
• A Peripheral Road of approximately 41 kilometres; and
• A Link Road of approximately 9.8 kilometres.
3. For implementation of the said project, extensive extents
of land measuring 20,193 acres situated in Bengaluru Urban
and surrounding areas were notified for acquisition under
Section 28(1) of the KIAD Act between the years 1998 and
2009. The final declarations under Section 28(4) were
subsequently issued between the years 2003 and 2009.
4. The petitioners before writ Court are the owners of
various extents of land situated in different villages notified for
acquisition under the KIAD Act. Though the final notifications
were issued between the years 2003 and 2009, no awards have
been passed in respect of the petitioners' lands even till date.

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
231
5. The BMICP and the land acquisition proceedings have
been the subject matter of several rounds of litigation before
this Court and the Supreme Court. The legality of the project,
the FWA, the public purpose underlying the acquisition and the
power of the State to acquire lands for the project were upheld
by the Supreme Court in a series of decisions including in the
case of STATE OF KARNATAKA vs ALL INDIA
MANUFACTURERS ORGANISATION [(2006) 4 SCC 683]
and in the case of M. NAGABHUSHANA vs STATE OF
KARNATAKA [(2011) 3 SCC 408]. Consequently, the validity
of the acquisition notifications and the public purpose
underlying the project attained finality.
6. Notwithstanding the conclusion of the earlier litigations,
several landowners, whose lands had been notified for
acquisition, approached this Court by filing the aforesaid writ
petitions. Their grievance was not directed against the legality
of the acquisition notifications or the validity of the BMICP
itself. Instead, they contended that although several decades
had elapsed after issuance of the acquisition notifications, the
acquiring authorities had failed to pass awards determining
compensation in respect of their lands. As a consequence, the
acquisition proceedings had remained incomplete for a period

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
232
of more than 23 years since compensation had neither been
determined nor paid.
7. The petitioners asserted that such a prolonged inaction on
the part of the acquiring authorities had placed them in an
extremely prejudicial position. Their lands continued to remain
under acquisition, thereby substantially restricting them from
alienating, developing, cultivating, mortgaging or otherwise to
deal with their lands. At the same time, the State had failed to
discharge its statutory obligation of determining compensation.
The petitioners asserted that compulsory acquisition of their
lands without determination of award or payment of
compensation for decades rendered the acquisition proceedings
arbitrary, oppressive and contrary to the rights guaranteed
under Article 300A of the Constitution of India.
8. The petitioners further contended that several parcels of
lands acquired from them had not been utilised for the core
components of the BMICP viz., the expressway, peripheral
road, link road, toll plazas or interchanges. It was specifically
urged that despite acquisition of vast extents of land,
substantial portions remained unutilised for the said project for
which they had originally been acquired.

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
233
9. Upon consideration of the above submissions, the learned
Single Judge has held that though more than two decades had
elapsed from the date of issuance of the acquisition
notifications, however, no award has been passed. Placing
reliance upon the decisions of the Division Bench of this Court
in the case of KARNATAKA INDUSTRIAL AREAS
DEVELOPMENT BOARD vs K.H. SHIVANNA
[W.A.No.557/2017] as also in the case of SPECIAL LAND
ACQUISITION OFFICER, KIADB vs SMT. M.
SHAKUNTHALAMMA AND OTHERS [W.A.No.6763/2017]
which was affirmed by the Supreme Court in SLP(C)
No.3767/2024, wherein prolonged delay in passing awards had
been held fatal to acquisition proceedings, the learned Single
Judge concluded that the acquisition proceedings could not be
permitted to remain pending indefinitely. Accordingly, the
acquisition notifications were quashed insofar as they related to
the lands belonging to the petitioners.
10. Aggrieved by the said order dated 04.07.2025, the
appellant-NICE has preferred the present batch of writ appeals.
III SALIENT FEATURES OF THE FWA:
11. Integrated Infrastructure Corridor: The FWA envisages
the BMICP as an integrated infrastructure development project

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
234
and not merely as a road construction scheme. The project was
conceived with the objective of facilitating the orderly
development of Bengaluru by decongesting traffic, improving
connectivity between Bengaluru and Mysuru, promoting
industrial and commercial growth, generating employment and
creating planned urban centres along the corridor. The FWA,
therefore, treats the transportation infrastructure and urban
development as inseparable components of a single integrated
project.
12. Components of the Project: The FWA contemplates that
the project would comprise not only construction of the
expressway, peripheral roads and link roads, but also
construction of 5 self-sustaining townships together with
supporting infrastructure such as, power generation, water
supply, sewage treatment, telecommunications, waste
management and other civic amenities. The acquisition,
finance, design, construction and implementation of all these
components collectively constitute the infrastructure corridor
envisaged under the FWA.
13. Construction of 5 Self-Sustaining Townships: The FWA
specifically contemplates construction of 5 self-sustaining
townships viz., Township Nos.1, 2, 4, 5 and 7 at the

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
235
demarcated locations identified in the Project Technical Report
(PTR). The original proposal for 7 townships was reduced to 5
in the final FWA to ensure planned and orderly regional
development. Each township was assigned a distinct functional
character such as, Corporate Centre, Industrial Centre, Eco-tourism Centre, Heritage Centre and Commercial Centre and
was required to be developed only within the extent of land
earmarked for that purpose. The townships were conceived as
integral components of the infrastructure corridor and not as
independent real estate developments. The PTR envisages the
5 townships with distinct functional identities, the salient
features of which are as follows:-
i) Township No. 1 – Corporate Township (2,792 acres):
Situated on the outskirts of Bengaluru along both
sides of the Expressway near the Outer Ring Road,
Township No.1 was conceived as a Corporate
Township. It was intended to house research and
development centres, business establishments,
hotels, a golf course and residential areas together
with hospitals, educational institutions and other civic
infrastructures. The township was designed as a self-contained urban centre with due regard to

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
236
environmental protection through green belts and
planned development.
ii) Township No. 2 – Commercial Township (1,868 acres):
Located approximately 10 kilometres from the
Bengaluru urban boundary, Township No.2 was
envisaged as a Commercial Township aimed at
reducing urbanisation pressures on Bengaluru. The
township was planned around a central commercial
and institutional hub comprising of business centres,
hotels, hospitals, educational institutions and public
offices, with self-contained residential neighbourhoods
supported by parks, schools, health care facilities and
environmentally sustainable transport system.
iii) Township No. 4 – Industrial Township (1,660 acres):
Situated approximately 36 kilometres in the west of
Bengaluru along the proposed Expressway, Township
No.4 was planned as an Industrial Township. It was
intended to accommodate various industrial
establishments with supporting public infrastructure,
green buffers and environmental safeguards, while
providing modern industrial facilities with direct
access to the Expressway.

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
237
iv) Township No. 5 – Cultural and Residential Township
(2,700 acres):
Located near State Highway No.17 and the existing
railway line, Township No.5 was conceived as a
Cultural and Residential Township. The proposal
included a fully equipped medical centre, hospitals,
institutions for study of various systems of medicines,
centres for religious and vedic studies together with
residential development and supporting civic
infrastructure.
v)Township No. 7 – Eco-Tourism Township (4,010 acres):
Situated approximately 3 kilometres from the Mysuru
city and close to Kaveri River, Township No.7 was
conceived as an Eco-Tourism Township. It was
proposed to include amusement parks, golf courses,
hotels, residential neighbourhoods, commercial
establishments and public infrastructure, with the
overall objective of promoting eco-tourism through
planned urban development.
14. Nature and Composition of the Townships: The FWA
envisages that each township would be a self-sustaining urban
settlement and not merely a housing layout. Accordingly, every

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
238
township was required to include not only residential
development, but also schools, hospitals, shopping complexes,
parks, open spaces, roads, water supply, electricity, sewage,
conservancy and other socio-economic infrastructures. The
objective was to ensure that each township function as a
complete urban centre capable of supporting residential,
commercial and industrial activities without depending on the
existing urban infrastructure of Bengaluru.
15. Mandatory compliance with the FWA and PTR: The FWA
mandates that every component of the project be implemented
strictly in accordance with the specifications contained in the
FWA read with the PTR. The land acquired for the project is
required to be utilised only for the purposes contemplated
therein and the project proponents are not at liberty to alter
the location, nature or extent of the project components
unilaterally. Any deviation from the approved land use,
specifications or scope of work is permissible only upon
obtaining the prior approval of the State Government.
16. Binding Nature of the obligations under the FWA: The
Supreme Court has recognised that the rights of the project
proponents to implement the BMICP flow primarily from the
FWA and that the FWA defines both the scope of the project

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
239
and the obligations of the parties. It was held that neither the
supplementary agreements executed in the years 1999 and
2000 nor the Tripartite Agreement dated 09.08.2002 have
diluted or modified the obligation to establish 5 self-sustaining
townships at the designated locations. Consequently, the
project proponents remain bound to execute the project only in
the manner envisaged under the FWA read with the PTR and
any departure therefrom can be undertaken only with the
approval contemplated under the FWA.
IV FINDINGS OF THE LEARNED SINGLE JUDGE:
17. The learned Single Judge held that the issue arose in the
writ petitions was no longer res integra in view of the
judgments of the Division Bench of this Court in the cases of
K.H. SHIVANNA (supra) and SMT. SHAKUNTHALAMMA
(supra), wherein it was held that the acquisition proceedings
were liable to be quashed on account of inordinate delay in
passing the awards. The learned Single Judge further held that
the judgment in SHAKUNTHALAMMA (supra) was affirmed by
the Supreme Court and had, therefore, attained the finality. It
was also held that the delay in passing the awards in the case
on hand is far more than the delay considered in the cases of
K.H.SHIVANNA (supra) and SHAKUNTHALAMMA (supra).

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
240
The learned Single Judge, therefore, concluded that the
acquisition proceedings had remained incomplete for an
unreasonably long period and could not be permitted to
continue indefinitely.
18. While considering the objection raised by the NICE
regarding the earlier judgments of the Supreme Court
upholding the BMICP, the learned Single Judge has held that
the present challenge was not directed against the validity of
the project, the FWA or the acquisition notifications, instead,
the challenge was to the subsequent failure on the part of the
authorities to pass the awards despite lapse of decades.
19. Placing reliance on the decision of the Supreme Court in
the case of ANIL KUMAR GUPTA vs STATE OF BIHAR AND
OTHERS [(2012) 12 SCC 443], wherein it was held that the
acquisition proceedings may give rise to fresh and distinct
cause of action at different stages, the learned Single Judge
held that the cause of action in the case on hand arose from
prolonged inaction on the part of the acquiring authorities in
passing the awards and not from the original acquisition
notifications.

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
241
20. The learned Single Judge has observed that the lands in
question had not been utilised for expressway, peripheral road,
link road, toll plazas or interchanges forming part of the BMICP.
21. The learned Single Judge observed that the petitioners
had been subjected to acquisition proceedings for decades
without determination of compensation and the prolonged
pendency of the acquisition proceedings had adversely affected
their rights and kept them under uncertainty for an
unreasonable length of time. Therefore, the learned Single
Judge quashed the acquisition proceedings insofar as the
petitioners’ lands were concerned.

IX POINTS FOR CONSIDERATION:
44. Having considered the aforesaid submissions, the
following points arise for consideration in the present appeals:
1. Whether the acquisition proceedings, though validly
initiated under the provisions of the KIAD Act and
culminating in statutory vesting, can nevertheless be
sustained when the acquiring authorities have failed
to determine the compensation by passing the awards
for over two decades?
2. Whether the material placed on record regarding
implementation of the BMICP disclosing deviations

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
255
from the FWA is sufficient to disentitle the appellant-NICE from seeking interference with the order passed
by the learned Single Judge in exercise of the
appellate jurisdiction?
3. Whether the earlier judgments of the Supreme Court
upholding the validity of the BMICP and the
acquisition proceedings thereon, operate as a bar to
challenge the acquisition on the principles of res
judicata and doctrine of finality of litigation?
X ANALYSIS AND FINDINGS:
45. Re. Point No.1: The acquisition of the petitioners' lands
forms part of the BMICP, a project conceived for the
development of an integrated infrastructure corridor connecting
Bengaluru and Mysuru. The acquisition was undertaken under
the provisions of the KIAD Act for implementation of the project
in furtherance of a public purpose. The FWA dated 03.04.1997
executed between the State Government and the NICE-project
proponent constituted the basis upon which the project was
conceptualized, delineating the scope of the project, the
obligations of the project proponent and the broad manner in
which the project was to be implemented.

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
256
46. At the outset, it is necessary to ascertain the actual
dispute which requires consideration in the present appeals.
Much emphasis has been laid by the learned Senior Counsels
appearing for the appellant-NICE on the legal consequences
flowing from sub-section (4), (5) and (6) of Section 28 of the
KIAD Act. It has been repeatedly asserted that once the final
declaration is issued under Section 28(4) and possession is
taken in accordance with the KIAD Act, the land vests
absolutely in the State Government free from all encumbrances
and such vesting is complete and irreversible. It is further
submitted that the KIAD Act does not prescribe any period
within which an award is required to be passed and, therefore,
mere delay in determining the compensation cannot invalidate
the acquisition proceedings.
47. The above submission, though plausible at the first
glance, proceeds on the assumption that statutory vesting is
the culmination of the acquisition process. In our considered
opinion, this argument overlooks an equally significant facet of
compulsory acquisition i.e., the constitutional obligation of the
State to determine and pay the compensation within a
reasonable period. The question before us is not whether the
acquisition notifications were validly issued or whether the

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
257
lands stood vested absolutely in the State Government under
the KIAD Act. Those issues have attained finality in the earlier
rounds of litigation. The real question which arises in the
present appeals is whether the State, having invoked the power
of eminent domain and deprived the citizens of their property,
can indefinitely postpone the determination of compensation
without any explanation and still insists that the acquisition
must remain operative solely because vesting has taken place.
The distinction assumes considerable importance. The
grievance projected by the petitioners-landowners is entirely
different. It is founded upon the admitted failure of the
acquiring authorities to complete the acquisition proceedings by
determining the compensation despite lapse of extra-ordinarily
long period after issuance of the acquisition notifications.
48. The fact of non-passing of the award by the acquiring
authorities is not in dispute. As mentioned above, the
preliminary notifications under Section 28(1) of the KIAD Act
came to be issued between 1998 to 2009, while the
corresponding final declarations under Section 28(4) were
issued between 2003 and 2009. The lands belonging to the
petitioners formed part of these notifications. Despite passage
of time ranging to more than 23 years, admittedly, no awards

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
258
determining the compensation have been passed in respect of
the lands belonging to the petitioners. The learned Single Judge
has specifically recorded these undisputed facts which have not
been disputed before us either by NICE or the KIADB.
49. The consequence of such prolonged inaction cannot be
underestimated. From the date of issuance of the notifications,
the petitioners have remained under continuous disability in
relation to their properties. Their rights stood severely
restricted and their ability to alienate, develop, cultivate,
mortgage or otherwise to deal with the lands became
substantially impaired. At the same time, payment of
compensation, which constitutes indispensable consequence of
compulsory acquisition, remained undetermined. Thus, while
the State asserts that the lands have vested absolutely in it,
the landowners have neither retained the ownership nor
received the compensation which the law contemplates as the
inevitable corollary of compulsory acquisition. Such a state of
affairs, if permitted to continue indefinitely, would reduce the
constitutional rights guaranteed under Article 300A to a mere
formality.
50. It is true that the KIAD Act does not prescribe a specific
period within which an award is required to be passed.

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
259
However, the absence of an express statutory limitation cannot
be construed as conferring an unrestricted licence upon the
acquiring authority to postpone the passing of the award
indefinitely. It is a settled principle of the administrative law
that whenever a statute confers power upon a public authority
without prescribing any period for its exercise, such power
must nevertheless be exercised within a reasonable time.
Reasonableness is an inbuilt limitation and the public
authorities cannot keep such power in abeyance indefinitely to
the prejudice of the citizens. The acceptance of contrary
proposition would result in the State initiating acquisition
proceedings, depriving the landowners from enjoying their
properties and thereafter, postponing determination of
compensation for decades merely because the statute does not
expressly prescribe the limitation period. Such an interpretation
would be wholly inconsistent with the constitutional governance
and the rule of law.
51. The Supreme Court, while interpreting Article 300A of the
Constitution, has consistently emphasised that deprivation of
the property of the landowner cannot be sustained merely by
reference to the statutory authority. The procedure adopted by
the State must also satisfy the requirements of fairness,

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
260
reasonableness and non-arbitrariness. The right to property,
though no longer a fundamental right, continues to enjoy the
constitutional protection under Article 300A. The power of
compulsory acquisition is, therefore, accompanied by a
corresponding obligation to ensure that the owner receives just
compensation through a fair and expeditious process. The
constitutional balance lies not merely in the power to acquire,
but equally in the obligation to compensate the landowner.
52. In the case of K.T. PLANTATION PVT. LTD. vs STATE
OF KARNATAKA [(2011) 9 SCC 1], the Constitution Bench of
the Supreme Court recognized that Article 300A embodies a
constitutional limitation upon the State's power to deprive a
person of his property. Likewise, in the case of VIDYA DEVI vs
STATE OF HIMACHAL PRADESH [(2020) 2 SCC 569], the
Supreme Court reiterated that the right to property remains a
valuable constitutional right and that the State cannot
appropriate the private property except in accordance with a
just, fair and reasonable procedure. Again, in the case of HARI
KRISHNA MANDIR TRUST vs STATE OF MAHARASHTRA
[(2020) 9 SCC 356], it has been held that the State cannot
deprive the citizens of their property by adopting procedures
which are arbitrary or unfair. These authorities demonstrate

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
261
that compulsory acquisition is not merely a statutory exercise
of power, but one must conform to the constitutional standards
of fairness throughout.
53. This Court, in the case of SRI MUKESH KUMAR vs
STATE OF KARNATAKA AND OTHERS
[W.A.No.4647/2013], has explained the process of
acquisition and disposal of lands as provided under Sections 28
and 29 of the KIAD Act. The relevant paragraphs of the said
judgment are extracted hereunder:
“18. The acquisition and disposal of land is
provided in Chapter VII of the KIAD Act.
Sections 28 and 29, which are relevant,
are extracted hereunder:-
"28. Acquisition of land.- (1) If at any
time, in the opinion of the State
Government, any land is required for the
purpose of development by the Board, or
for any other purpose in furtherance of the
objects of this Act, the State Government
may by notification, give notice of its
intention to acquire such land.
(2) On publication of a notification under
subsection (1), the State Government shall
serve notice upon the owner or where the
owner is not the occupier, on the occupier
of the land and on all such persons known
or believed to be interested therein to
show cause, within thirty days from the
date of service of the notice, why the land
should not be acquired.

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
262
(3) After considering the cause, if any,
shown by the owner of the land and by
any other person interested therein, and
after giving such owner and person an
opportunity of being heard, the State
Government may pass such orders as it
deems fit.
(4) After orders are passed under sub-section (3), where the State Government
is satisfied that any land should be
acquired for the purpose specified in the
notification issued under sub-section (1), a
declaration shall, by notification in the
official Gazette, be made to that effect.
(5) On the publication in the official
Gazette of the declaration under sub-section (4), the land shall vest absolutely
in the State Government free from all
encumbrances.
(6) Where any land is vested in the State
Government under sub-section (5), the
State Government may, by notice in
writing, order any person who may be in
possession of the land to surrender or
deliver possession thereof to the State
Government or any person duly authorised
by it in this behalf within thirty days of the
service of the notice.
(7) If any person refuses or fails to comply
with an order made under sub-section (5),
the State Government or any officer
authorised by the State Government in
this behalf may take possession of the
land and may for that purpose use such
force as may be necessary.
(8) Where the land has been acquired for
the Board, the State Government, after it
has taken possession of the land, may
transfer the land to the Board for the

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
263
purpose for which the land has been
acquired.
29. Compensation- (1) Where any land is
acquired by the State Government under
this Chapter, the State Government shall
pay for such acquisition compensation in
accordance with the provisions of this Act.
(2) Where the amount of compensation
has been determined by agreement
between the State Government and the
person to be compensated, it shall be paid
in accordance with such agreement.
(3) Where no such agreement can be
reached, the State Government shall refer
the case to the Deputy Commissioner for
determination of the amount of
compensation to be paid for such
acquisition as also the person or persons
to whom such compensation shall be paid.
(4) On receipt of a reference under sub-section (3), the Deputy Commissioner
shall serve notice on the owner or occupier
of such land and on all persons known or
believed to be interested herein to appear
before him and state their respective
interests in the said land."
19. Section 28 provides a complete
machinery in respect of the method and
manner in which the land can be acquired
by the State Government, non-compliance
of any of the provisions would render the
acquisition invalid, illegal and fraud on
statute. Sub-section (1) of Section 28
specifically provides that the State
Government may acquire the land for the
purpose of development by the Board or
for any other purpose in furtherance of the
objects of the Act. If the land is not
acquired either for the purpose of

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
264
development by the Board or for any other
purpose in furtherance of the objects of
the Act, such acquisition is nothing but a
mala fide and illegal and colourable
exercise of power and a fraud on statute
and Constitution in exercising the eminent
domain by the State.
20. Sub-section (2) of Section 28 provides
an opportunity for filing objections by the
landowners or other interested persons
and sub-section (3) provides for
considering the objections and affording an
opportunity of hearing to them. After the
aforesaid exercise is undertaken, then only
the final notification is to be issued under
sub-section (4) of Section 28 of the Act.
Under sub-section (5), it is provided that
on publication of the final notification
under sub-section (4), the land vests
absolutely in the State Government free
from all encumbrances, but sub-section
(6) requires the Government Authorities to
serve a notice in writing and order on the
person who is in possession of the land to
surrender or deliver the possession thereof
to the State Government within thirty days
of the service of the notice. Sub-section
(7) provides that if such a person refuses
or fails to comply with the order made
under sub-section (5), the State
Government may take possession of the
land by using the force as may be
necessary.
21. Under Section 29 of the KIAD Act, the
Government is under a statutory and
constitutional obligation to pay adequate
compensation in accordance with the
provisions of the Act for the land acquired,
and if the landowner is not satisfied with
the compensation, the reference has to be
made to the Deputy Commissioner for

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
265
determination of the amount of
compensation to be paid for such
acquisition. Before deciding the reference,
the Deputy Commissioner is required to
serve a notice on the owner and on all
such persons who are interested in the
land, to appear before him and to state
their respective interests in the land so
acquired.
22. Thus, Sections 28 and 29 provide a
complete mechanism for acquiring the land
under the provisions of the KIAD Act i.e.,
issuing the notification under Section
28(1), filing the objections, considering
the objections, providing opportunity of
hearing, issuing the final notification under
Section 28(4), issuing notice to surrender
or deliver possession of the land to the
State Government and thereafter, for
payment of compensation. All the steps
are to be taken in the manner as provided
for in the statute and if time has not been
granted for filing the objections or the
objectors have not been heard or no notice
has been issued for surrendering
possession or no compensation has been
paid, then the land acquisition proceedings
cannot be said to be complete and would
be susceptible to be declared illegal and
ultra vires the statute and the Constitution
of India being in violation of Articles 14, 21
and 300A of the Constitution.”
54. It is one thing to hold that vesting is complete whereas, it
is altogether another to hold that once vesting takes place, the
State is relieved of its continuing statutory and constitutional
obligation to determine and pay the compensation within
reasonable time. The concept of vesting cannot be expanded to

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
266
obliterate the corresponding duty cast upon the acquiring
authority. Vesting answers the question of title. It does not
answer the question whether the State can indefinitely
postpone payment of compensation after depriving the citizens
of their property.
55. If the submissions advanced on behalf of the appellant-NICE were to be accepted, it would result in startling
consequences. This means that although the State acquires
the property compulsorily and the owner loses all effective
incidents of ownership, the determination of compensation
could be deferred indefinitely without any legal consequences.
Such an interpretation would render the constitutional right
guaranteed under Article 300A entirely illusory and would
permit arbitrary deprivation of property without temporal
limitation. We are unable to attribute such an intention either
to the Legislature or to the constitutional scheme.
56. No satisfactory explanation has been placed on record
justifying such an inordinate delay. The only explanation given
is that the project was embroiled in prolonged litigations. While
the earlier litigation may explain the delay during a particular
period, it cannot constitute a blanket justification for decades of
inaction, particularly after the legality of the project and the

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
267
acquisition had been upheld by the Supreme Court. Once the
impediments arising from the litigation stood removed, the
statutory authorities were expected to proceed with expedition
in determining the compensation. The failure on the part of the
authorities to do so cannot now be justified by relying upon the
very delay occasioned by their own inaction.
57. The Division Bench of this Court, in K.H. SHIVANNA
(supra), has considered a delay of approximately 9 years in
passing the award and held that such a delay is unreasonable.
Subsequently, in M. SHAKUNTHALAMMA (supra), the delay of
11 years in passing the award was considered and the
acquisition proceedings were quashed, which decision has
admittedly been affirmed by the Supreme Court. However, the
delay involved in the present batch of appeals is substantially
greater i.e, beyond two decades.
58. The absence of an express limitation cannot be equated
with the absence of a legal obligation. A statutory authority is
expected to exercise every statutory power diligently, fairly and
within a reasonable period, more particularly when the exercise
of such power has a direct bearing upon the constitutional
rights of citizens. The doctrine of "reasonable time" is not an
external principle sought to be engrafted upon the statute; it is

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
268
an inherent limitation upon every statutory power unless the
Legislature expressly provides otherwise. The Supreme Court
has repeatedly held that where a statute is silent as to the time
within which a power is to be exercised, the authority is
nevertheless required to act within a reasonable period. The
requirement of reasonableness thus permeates every
administrative action. This principle assumes even greater
significance in matters of compulsory acquisition, where the
State exercises the extraordinary power of eminent domain.
59. The paragraphs of the judgment of the Constitution
Bench in the case of K.T. PLANTATION (supra) which is
revelant for the case on hand is extracted hereunder:
"Public purpose
180. Deprivation of property within the meaning
of Article 300A, generally speaking, must take
place for public purpose or public interest. The
concept of eminent domain which applies when a
person is deprived of his property postulates that
the purpose must be primarily public and not
primarily of private interest and merely
incidentally beneficial to the public. Any law,
which deprives a person of his private property
for private interest, will be unlawful and unfair
and undermines the rule of law and can be
subjected to judicial review. But the question as
to whether the purpose is primarily public or
private, has to be decided by the legislature,
which of course should be made known.
181. The concept of public purpose has been
given fairly expansive meaning which has to be
justified upon the purpose and object of the

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
269
statute and the policy of the legislation. Public
purpose is, therefore, a condition precedent, for
invoking Article 300A. Compensation
182. We have found that the requirement of
public purpose is invariably the rule for depriving
a person of his property, violation of which is
amenable to judicial review. Let us now examine
whether the requirement of payment of
compensation is the rule after the deletion of
Article 31(2).
183. Payment of compensation amount is a
constitutional requirement under Article 30(1- A)
and under the second proviso to Article 31-A(1),
unlike Article 300- A. After the Forty-fourth
Amendment Act, 1978, the constitutional
obligation to pay compensation to a person who
is deprived of his property primarily depends
upon the terms of the statute and the legislative
policy. Article 300A, however, does not prohibit
the payment of just compensation when a
person is deprived of his property, but the
question is whether a person is entitled to get
compensation, as a matter of right, in the
absence of any stipulation in the statute,
depriving him of his property.
189. Requirement of public purpose, for
deprivation of a person of his property under
Article 300-A, is a precondition, but no
compensation or nil compensation or its
illusiveness has to be justified by the State on
judicially justiciable standards. Measures
designed to achieve greater social justice, may
call for lesser compensation and such a limitation
by itself will not make legislation invalid or
unconstitutional or confiscatory. In other words,
the right to claim compensation or the obligation
to pay, though not expressly included in Article
300-A, it can be inferred in that article and it is
for the State to justify its stand on justifiable
grounds which may depend upon the legislative
policy, object and purpose of the statute and
host of other factors.
190. Article 300-A would be equally violated if
the provisions of law authorizing deprivation of
property have not been complied with. While
enacting Article 300-A Parliament has only

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
270
borrowed Article 31(1) (the "Rule of law"
doctrine) and not Article 31(2) (which had
embodied the doctrine of eminent domain).
Article 300-A enables the State to put
restrictions on the right to property by law. That
law has to be reasonable. It must comply with
other provisions of the Constitution. The
limitation or restriction should not be arbitrary or
excessive or what is beyond what is required in
public interest. The limitation or restriction must
not be disproportionate to the situation or
excessive.
191. The legislation providing for deprivation of
property under Article 300-A must be "just, fair
and reasonable" as understood in terms of
Articles 14, 19(1)(g), 26(b), 301, etc. Thus in
each case, courts will have to examine the
scheme of the impugned Act, its object, purpose
as also the question whether payment of nil
compensation or nominal compensation would
make the impugned law unjust, unfair or
unreasonable in terms of other provisions of the
Constitution as indicated above. 192. At this
stage, we may clarify that there is a difference
between "no" compensation and "nil"
compensation. A law seeking to acquire private
property for public purpose cannot say that "no
compensation shall be paid". However, there
could be a law awarding "nil" compensation in
cases where the State undertakes to discharge
the liabilities charged on the property under
acquisition and onus is on the Government to
establish validity of such law. In the latter case,
the court in exercise of judicial review will test
such a law keeping in mind the above
parameters.
193. Right to property no more remains an
overarching guarantee in our Constitution, then
is it the law, that such a legislation enacted
under the authority of law as provided in Article
300-A is immune from challenge before a
constitutional court for violation of Articles 14, 21
or the overarching principle of the rule of law, a
basic feature of our Constitution, especially when
such a right is not specifically incorporated in
Article 300-A, unlike Article 30(1- A) and the
second proviso to Article 31-A(1)"

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
271
60. The aforesaid principles have been reiterated by the
Supreme Court in the case of KOLKATA MUNICIPAL
CORPORATION AND ANOTHER vs BIMAL KUMAR SHAH
AND OTHERS [(2024) 10 SCC 533] and further held that the
right to property has to be given broader and meaningful
interpretation. The right to property has seven sub-rights and it
does not limit to twin components of public purpose and
compensation. The seven sub-rights or strands constituting the
right to property, which are non-exhaustive, have been
enumerated as under:-
"(i) The duty of the State to inform the person
that it intends to acquire his property - the right
to notice,
(ii) The duty of the State to hear objections to
the acquisition - the right to be heard,
(iii) The duty of the State to inform the person of
its decision to acquire - the right to a reasoned
decision,
(iv) The duty of the State to demonstrate that
the acquisition is for public purpose - the duty to
acquire only for public purpose,
(v) The duty of the State to restitute and
rehabilitate - the right of restitution or fair
compensation,
(vi) The duty of the State to conduct the process
of acquisition efficiently and within prescribed
timelines of the proceedings - the right to an
efficient and expeditious process, and
(vii) The final conclusion of the proceedings
leading to vesting - the right of conclusion.”

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
272
61. In view of the aforesaid discussions, we are of the
considered view that although the lands stood vested in the
State under the provisions of the KIAD Act, such vesting does
not dispense with the statutory and constitutional obligation of
the acquiring authorities to determine and pay the
compensation within a reasonable time. The admitted failure on
the part of the authorities to pass the awards for more than
two decades coupled with the absence of any satisfactory
explanation for such an inordinate delay renders the acquisition
proceedings unsustainable. The learned Single Judge has,
therefore, committed no error in quashing the acquisition
proceedings insofar as they relate to the petitioners' lands.
Hence, the point No.1 answered in affirmative and against the
appellants.
62. Re. Point No.2: While deciding point No.1, we have held
that the inordinate and unexplained delay extending over two
decades in passing the awards is by itself sufficient to sustain
the conclusion reached by the learned Single Judge. However,
the matter does not rest there. The petitioners have produced
considerable material to demonstrate the manner in which the
BMICP has been implemented which has materially deviated

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
273
from the public purpose for which the lands were originally
acquired. Since the appellant-NICE has repeatedly urged that
the acquisition must be protected on the ground that it forms
part of a major public infrastructure project, it becomes
necessary to examine whether the material now placed before
this Court lends support to that submission.
63. It is well settled that a party seeking equitable relief
before the Court must demonstrate fairness in implementing
the project. Where the conduct of the acquiring authorities or
the beneficiary of the acquisition gives rise to a substantial
doubt regarding the adherence to the very public purpose for
which compulsory acquisition was undertaken, the Court cannot
ignore such circumstances while exercising the appellate
jurisdiction, under the Article 226 of the Constitution of India.
64. In the case of BANGALORE MYSORE
INFRASTRUCTURE CORRIDOR AREA PLANNING
AUTHORITY & ANR. vs NANDI INFRASTRUCTURE
CORRIDOR ENTERPRISE LTD. & ORS. [(2021) 18 SCC
401], the Supreme Court has critically evaluated whether a
project proponent could unilaterally modify the structural terms
of the FWA and held that the BMICP was governed strictly by
the FWA and PTR, which explicitly mandated the creation of 5

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
274
self-sustained townships at specified locations. The Supreme
Court emphasized that under Article 7.1 of the FWA, any
deviation or modification from the agreed upon plan requires
prior written approval from the State Government or its
designated Empowered Committee. Bypassing this hierarchy to
seek building permission directly from the BMICP Authority
rendered the application legally non-existent (non est).
Furthermore, the Supreme Court has addressed the argument
regarding land use permissions under the Outline Development
Plan (ODP) or Master Plan. It was held that the classification of
land as residential under the Master Plan does not, by itself,
permit the project proponent to develop standalone residential
projects on the land acquired for the specific purpose of
implementing the BMICP under the FWA. The Supreme Court
concluded that land use classifications in an ODP cannot
override the contractual obligations and intended spatial design
set out in the FWA and PTR.
65. Mr. H.N. Shashidhara, learned Senior Counsel appearing
for the landowners has produced before this Court, a report
prepared by the Institute for Social and Economic Change
(ISEC) which is an All India Institute for Interdisciplinary
Research and Training in the Social Sciences getting grant-in-

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
275
aid from the Government of Karnataka and which works with
the Central, State and Local Governments as well as
international agencies. He has also placed before us several
registered sale deeds, sale agreements, Joint Development
Agreements, annual reports and financial statements of the
appellant-NICE covering the period from 2008-09 to 2023-24.
However, the principal submission of the appellant-NICE is that
these materials are irrelevant since the legality of the
acquisition already stands concluded. We are unable to accept
such a broad proposition.
66. The relevance of these documents does not lie in re-opening the legality of the acquisition notifications. Rather,
they throw light upon the manner in which the lands were
acquired by invoking the power of eminent domain and whether
the implementation of the project has remained consistent with
the public purpose originally projected before the Court. One of
the principal circumstances emerging from the ISEC Report
relates to the extent of land acquired for implementation of the
project. The FWA contemplated acquisition of 20,193 acres of
land for the BMICP. Out of this, 6,999 acres were earmarked
for road components viz., Expressway, Peripheral Road and
Link Road, while 13,194 acres were earmarked for 5 proposed

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
276
townships. The report, however, records that the subsequent
planning documents, particularly the Outline Development Plan
dated 12.02.2004 has altered this distribution by increasing the
land requirement for the road components to 9,471 acres,
while correspondingly reducing the land earmarked for the
township component by 2,472 acres. However, no satisfactory
explanation has been furnished for this substantial re-distribution of land between different project components and
the excess acquisition of land. Further, the affidavit filed by the
State before the Supreme Court in Contempt Petition (C)
No.139/2012 states that '554 acres of excess land has been
handed over to the appellant-NICE' (emphasis supplied).
The said affidavit was accepted and on the basis of the said
affidavit, the contempt proceedings were closed by the
Supreme Court vide order dated 13.07.2012.
67. The report further records that acquisition proceedings
ultimately extended to a substantially larger extent in several
villages situated in Bengaluru Urban and Ramanagara districts
than what was originally contemplated under the FWA.
According to the report, such enlargement facilitated
availability of additional parcels of land possessing significant
commercial value. Whether this conclusion ultimately withstand

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
277
the judicial scrutiny is not the immediate issue before us.
Nevertheless, these findings undoubtedly constitute relevant
circumstances while examining the question whether the
acquisition proceedings which have remained incomplete for
decades ought to be sustained or not.
68. The landowners have also relied upon several registered
transactions executed by the appellant-NICE in respect of the
lands forming part of the project. The documents placed before
this Court disclose Joint Development Agreements dated
31.03.2011 executed with Umang Realtech Private Limited
covering 10 acres 21 guntas situated at Madavara Village and
with Dhammanagi Developers Private Limited covering 20 acres
33 guntas situated at Kengeri Village. These agreements
pertain to multiple survey numbers that contemplate
commercial development of substantial extents of project land
through private developers.
69. Similarly, the material includes a registered Sale Deed
dated 14.01.2015 executed in favour of SBK Properties Private
Limited relating to 12 acres situated at Pillaganahalli Village
and a Sale Agreement dated 15.04.2015 executed with
Darshita Housing Private Limited covering 13 acres 30 guntas
in the very same village. Further, 3 Sale Agreements dated

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
278
16.07.2008 were executed with Ajmera Housing Corporation
relating to the lands situated in Varahasandra, B.M. Kaval and
KengeriGollahalli Villages covering an aggregate extent of
approximately 54 acres. Collectively, the documents placed
before this Court relate to approximately 110 acres of land
spread across Madavara, Kengeri, Pillaganahalli, Varahasandra,
B.M. Kaval and KengeriGollahalli Villages. It is not the
execution of these transactions alone which assumes
significance. Their cumulative effect indicates that the lands
acquired for implementation of a public infrastructure project
were subsequently made the subject matter of registered
conveyances, Joint Development Agreements and commercial
arrangements with the private developers. The financial
disclosures contained in the annual reports of NICE reinforce
this position.
70. The annual reports produced before us reveal that as
early as the financial year 2008-09, the Company disclosed
income from sale of land. By 2010-11, the Company had
disclosed receipt of advances towards sale of land and Joint
Development Agreements entered into with various parties. The
Annual Report for the year 2013-14 records that the Company
intended to mobilise financial resources through disposal of

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
279
developed land parcels and development of land on a Joint
Development basis. During the financial year 2014-15, the
Company disclosed receipt of approximately Rs.2,706.3 million
from the sale of developed land as against toll collections of
approximately Rs.1,879.2 million. Likewise, during 2015-16,
the financial statements disclose approximately Rs.27.48 crores
received from sale of developed land, while toll revenue stood
at approximately Rs.219.82 crores. Even in 2016-17, the
Company separately disclosed the income of approximately
Rs.16 crores from sale of the land. The subsequent balance
sheets consistently disclose the advances received towards the
sale of land and the Joint Development Agreements ranging
between Rs.36 crores and Rs.48 crores under non-current
liabilities together with advances of approximately Rs.56 crores
under other liabilities. These financial disclosures are not
isolated entries. They depict a continuing commercial pattern
extending over several financial years wherein sale of
developed land, Joint Development Agreements and
monetisation of project lands constituted recurring sources of
revenue alongside toll collections. Though these disclosures
may not by themselves determine the legality of every
transaction, they undoubtedly constitute relevant materials

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
280
while appreciating the manner in which the project has been
implemented.
71. The report also refers to several mortgage transactions,
lease arrangements and Joint Development Agreements
executed in respect of the acquired lands and records that the
project lands were utilised as security for raising finance from
banks and financial institutions. It further records that the
acquired lands became the subject matter of extensive
commercial development involving residential layouts,
commercial complexes, technology parks, hotels and other
revenue generating assets. These facts prima facie indicate that
developable land constituted an important commercial asset
forming part of the financial model adopted for implementation
of the BMICP.
72. The landowners have further placed reliance upon the
extracts from the Company's Annual Reports showing that
completed portions of the Link Road and Peripheral Road were
capitalised as assets in the financial statements of the
Company. The significance of such disclosures lies not in
conclusively establishing any illegality, but in appreciating
whether the implementation of the project has remained
confined to the public purpose for which compulsory acquisition

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
281
was undertaken. In our view, it is a fraud on the statute and
Constitution. The State authorities are accomplice in this fraud
and gross breaches and violations of the FWA. The proposed
project has not done any public good or served any public
purpose except to benefit the project proponent in
unimaginable properties. The project was though conceived for
public purpose, but it has served only private interests of the
project proponents.
73. Further, the appellant-NICE has violated the FWA by
increasing the toll fees on various occasions without seeking
the prior approval of the State which is evident from the
documents submitted by the State itself. The lakes and tank-bed lands of various villages have been handed over to the
appellant-NICE in violation of the judgment of the Supreme
Court in the case of HINCHLAL TIWARI vs KAMALA DEVI
AND OTHERS [(2001) 6 SCC 496], wherein it was held that
the material resources of the community like forests, tanks,
ponds, hillock, mountain etc. are nature's bounty. They
maintain delicate ecological balance. They need to be protected
for a proper and healthy environment which enables people to
enjoy a quality life which is the essence of the guaranteed right
under Article 21 of the Constitution. Viewed together, these

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
282
facts become relevant while considering whether the acquisition
continues to satisfy the public purpose for which the power of
eminent domain was invoked.
74. Another fact which cannot be ignored is the finding
recorded by the learned Single Judge that the landowners
specifically contended that many of the acquired lands had not
been utilised for the Expressway, Peripheral Road, Link Road,
toll plazas or inter-changes and that such submission remained
substantially undisputed by the otherside. The learned Single
Judge has taken note of this fact while appreciating the
prejudice caused to the landowners.
75. This Court cannot altogether ignore the material which
prima facie indicates that the lands acquired through
compulsory acquisition have, over a period of years, become
inter-twined with substantial commercial transactions extending
beyond construction of road infrastructure.The appellant-NICE
seeks to set aside the order passed by the learned Single Judge
primarily on the premise that the acquisition formed part of a
project conceived in larger public interest. However, the
implementation of that very project is accompanied by
commercial exploitation of acquired lands, alteration of land
utilisation, execution of Joint Development Agreements, sale

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
283
transactions and capitalisation of project assets. The material
produced before this Court lends support to the conclusion that
the order passed by the learned Single Judge does not call for
interference. We are, therefore, of the considered view that the
appellant-NICE has failed to establish any ground warranting
interference in exercise of the appellate jurisdiction.
Accordingly, we answer point No.2 against the appellants.
76. Re. Point No.3: The principal submission advanced on
behalf of the appellant-NICE is that the writ petitions were
liable to be rejected at the threshold since the acquisition
proceedings forming part of the BMICP have already been
upheld in the earlier rounds of litigation culminating in the
judgments of the Supreme Court. It is submitted that the
legality of the FWA, the public purpose underlying the
acquisition, the notifications issued under the KIAD Act and the
competence of the State Government to undertake acquisition
for implementation of the BMICP have all attained finality.
Consequently, according to the appellant, the landowners
cannot be permitted to reagitate these issues which stand
concluded by competent Court.
77. There is no dispute with the legal proposition that the
doctrine of res judicata is founded upon considerations of public

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
284
policy. A litigation must attain finality and parties cannot be
permitted to repeatedly re-open the issues which have already
been conclusively adjudicated by the Courts of competent
jurisdiction. The judgments of the Supreme Court rendered in
earlier rounds of litigation bind all the Courts.
78. However, the applicability of the doctrine depends not
merely upon the identity of parties but, more importantly on
the identity of the issue which is directly and substantially in
dispute. This principle does not prohibit every subsequent
proceedings arising out of the same transaction. It bars only
those proceedings in which the very issue already adjudicated
is sought to be re-opened. Where the subsequent events give
rise to an independent and distinct cause of action, the doctrine
has no application.
79. In ALL INDIA MANUFACTURERS ORGANISATION
(supra), the Supreme Court has examined the legality of the
FWA, the public purpose underlying the BMICP, the authority of
the State Government to undertake acquisition under the KIAD
Act and the overall validity of the project. Thereafter, in
M.NAGABHUSHANA (supra), the Supreme Court once again
considered the challenges to the very same acquisition
proceedings and upheld the legality of the acquisition. Thus,

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
285
the legal position that the acquisition notifications were validly
issued and that the project subserved a public purpose
undoubtedly has attained finality.
80. The present proceedings, however, do not seek
reconsideration of any of those issues. The landowners have
not challenged the competence of the State Government to
acquire the land for industrial purposes nor have they sought a
declaration that the preliminary or final notifications were void
on the grounds urged in the earlier litigations. The grievance of
the landowners is that although the acquisition proceedings
commenced between 1998 and 2009, despite lapse of two
decades, the acquiring authorities have failed to perform the
statutory obligation of determining the compensation by
passing the award. This prolonged inaction gave rise to a fresh
and independent cause of action, distinct from the legality of
the acquisition itself.
81. The validity of an acquisition is one issue. The legality of
continuing acquisition proceedings indefinitely without
determining the compensation is an altogether different issue.
Similarly, the compulsory acquisition originally undertaken for a
public purpose is one question, and whether, after acquisition,
the project has been implemented consistently with that public

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
286
purpose over the succeeding years is altogether another
question.
82. These questions could not have been possibly adjudicated
in the earlier rounds of litigation because the factual foundation
itself had not arisen then.
83. The Supreme Court, in the case of ANIL KUMAR GUPTA
(supra), while dealing with successive causes of action arising
from the acquisition proceedings, has recognized that different
stages of acquisition may furnish separate and distinct causes
of action. A challenge founded upon subsequent statutory
defaults cannot be rejected merely because an earlier challenge
to the acquisition notifications had failed. The learned Single
Judge has rightly relied upon the said principle while holding
that the present proceedings arise from the admitted failure to
pass awards over an extraordinarily long period and not from
the original acquisition notifications.
84. If every subsequent challenge was to be treated as
barred merely because the acquisition notifications had once
been upheld, the acquiring authority would be rendered
immune from judicial scrutiny irrespective of its conduct. If the
awards were not passed for several decades, compensation

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
287
remained unpaid indefinitely or statutory obligations were
persistently ignored, the landowners would be left remediless
solely because the original acquisition had survived judicial
review. Such a proposition cannot be accepted. The doctrine of
finality cannot be expanded to protect the subsequent
administrative inaction of statutory and constitutional frauds. It
protects judicial determinations and it does not immunise
future statutory defaults and criminal and civil liability.
85. The appellant-NICE has contended that permitting
repeated litigations would seriously impede the implementation
of public infrastructure projects. We are unable to accept this
submission in the facts of the present case. The delay
complained of by the landowners is not of few months or few
years. Such prolonged failure to complete the statutory
obligations cannot be characterised as an inevitable
consequence of litigations alone. Once the legality of the
acquisition stood affirmed by the Supreme Court way back in
2006 and 2011, it became incumbent upon the authorities to
proceed with reasonable expedition in determining the
compensation. Their subsequent omission cannot now be
shielded behind the doctrine of finality.

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
288
86. Besides the afore, the sale deeds, Joint Development
Agreements, annual reports, financial reports are all pertaining
to the events occurring after execution of the FWA and, in
several instances, years after the earlier litigation had
concluded. These materials were neither available for
consideration nor adjudicated upon in the earlier rounds of
litigation. Their relevance, therefore, cannot be excluded
merely by invoking the doctrine of res judicata.
87. We are, therefore, of the view that the present case do
not seek to re-open the findings recorded by the Supreme
Court regarding the validity of the BMICP, the acquisition
notifications or the land acquisition. They are founded upon a
subsequent and continuing failure of the acquiring authorities
to discharge their statutory obligations concerning the
implementation of the project. These constitute independent
causes of action which were neither directly nor substantially in
issue in the earlier rounds of proceedings, and protected under
Article 300A of Constitution. We answer this point too against
the appellants.
XI CONCLUSION:
88. The facts as disclosed would clearly suggest that there is
nothing nice about the NICE project except that the farmers

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
289
have been robbed of their lands and livelihood of generations
without payment of compensation as mandated under Article
300A of the Constitution of India. We have been informed that
out of 111 kms Expressway, the NICE has constructed only
5 kilometres Expressway in the last 25-26 years as per the
affidavit dated 28.07.2026 filed by Mr. Anjum Parwez,
Additional Chief Secretary, PWD. The NICE is sitting over huge
land bank of more than 20,000 acres, and the land and the
assets have been alienated and capitalized to its benefit as
stated above against the Supreme Court judgment in
BANGALORE MYSORE INFRASTRUCTURE CORRIDOR
AREA PLANNING AUTHORITY (supra) and in violation of the
terms of FWA. The State cannot be a party in concentrating
wealth in a private person, by exercising eminent domain at the
cost of the innocent citizens/farmers, as the same goes against
the Rule of law. This Court exercising its extra-ordinary
Jurisdiction cannot become a silent spectator, and allow any
illegality to perpetuate only on the ground that the earlier
challenges attained finality, when different circumstances were
placed.
89. By constructing the peripheral roads, putting up toll
plazas and collecting huge tolls, increasing the toll without prior

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
290
approval of the State in violation of the Framework Agreement
etc., the NICE is earning huge profits without fulfilling the
obligations under the FWA. The State itself has pointed out
several violations committed by the NICE in its operations.
90. The lakes, water bodies, waterbed liners have been
transferred to the NICE damaging the environment, ecology
and in detriment to the water supply chain of Bengaluru
particularly, and the neighbouring areas.
91. It appears that this BMICP may be one of the biggest
scam in the State of Karnataka and it demonstrates how a
State, which is the trustee of the natural resources on behalf of
the citizens, can allow the private interest to flourish in utter
violation of the constitutional mandate and the law to the
detriment of environment, ecology and against the public
interest for questionable considerations. One of the contentions
urged by the appellant-NICE is that the Government has not
given/acquired the extent of land as required under the FWA
and therefore, the Government has been in violation
of the FWA which has resulted in non-completion of the
project in 25 years. However, the said contention of
the appellant - NICE is not only false, but incorrect

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
291
which is evident from the affidavit filed on behalf of the State in
Contempt Petition (Civil) No.139/2012 that 554 acres of excess
land had been handed over to the appellant-NICE for the said
project.
92. We fail to understand why the State is not reviewing the
project or cancelling the same as the NICE has utterly failed in
its objective i.e., developing 5 Townships and Expressway of
approximately 111 kms., between the two major cities of
Karnataka i.e., Bengaluru and Mysuru to decongest the city of
Bengaluru and provide and create opportunities for
employment and growth alongside the Expressway.
93. The NICE project has done nothing but to benefit its
proponents in an astronomical way at the expense of the
landowners and the public interest in general. This is a fit case
where an independent assessment and investigation be carried
out by the special team of experts by conducting forensic audit
of its accounts and for committing fraud and other criminal acts
of commissions and omissions. However, we have doubt that
such an exercise will ever be taken up as the State itself is
accomplice in allowing the project proponents to earn huge
profits illegally by diverting the lands against the FWA,
capitalizing the assets, drying up the lakes, collecting huge toll

WA No. 1214 of 2025
AND Connected Matters
292
in violation of the FWA and sitting over the huge land bank
without payment of compensation etc. We, therefore, dismiss
these appeals filed by both the NICE and the KIADB and affirm
the judgment and order dated 04.07.2025 passed by the
learned Single Judge.
 In view of dismissal of the appeals, pending IAs, if any,
do not survive for consideration and accordingly, they stand
disposed of.
